Q: How do you check if 24 and 36 have a common factor of 2?

To check if 24 and 36 have a common factor of 2, you can divide both numbers by 2.

  • 24 ÷ 2 = 12
  • 36 ÷ 2 = 18

Since both 12 and 18 are whole numbers (no remainders), it means 2 is a common factor of 24 and 36.

So, yes, 24 and 36 have a common factor of 2!

Q: What is the difference between a common multiple and a common factor?

A common multiple is a number that is a multiple of two or more numbers at the same time. For example, 12 is a common multiple of 3 and 4 because 3 × 4 = 12 and 4 × 3 = 12.

A common factor, on the other hand, is a number that divides two or more numbers exactly without leaving a remainder. For example, 2 is a common factor of 4 and 6 because 4 ÷ 2 = 2 and 6 ÷ 2 = 3.

In simple words, common multiples are numbers that two numbers can both reach when jumping or multiplying, while common factors are numbers that can divide both numbers evenly.

Q: What does it mean if two numbers have no common multiples except 0?

When two numbers have no common multiples except 0, it means they don't share any other common multiples. In simple words, their only common multiple is 0 because they don't both fit into any other same multiple. Usually, this happens if the numbers are prime numbers and don't divide each other evenly. For example, 3 and 5 only both go into 0, so their only common multiple is 0.

Q: How do you find the common multiples of 3 and 6 using their tables?

To find the common multiples of 3 and 6 using their tables, first list the multiples of each number.

  • Multiples of 3: 3, 6, 9, 12, 15, 18, ...
  • Multiples of 6: 6, 12, 18, 24, 30, ...

Look for the numbers that appear in both lists. These are the common multiples. For example, 6, 12, 18, etc., are common multiples of both 3 and 6.

So, the common multiples are the numbers that show up in both tables!

Q: Can 1 be a common multiple of any two numbers? Why?

Yes, 1 can be a common multiple of any two numbers because any number can be divided by 1 without leaving a remainder. However, in terms of common multiples, we usually look for multiples that are larger than 1. The number 1 is called a "common multiple" only if both numbers are 1 or if we are starting from 0, since 0 is a multiple of every number. But generally, when we talk about common multiples, we look for numbers like 6, 12, or 24 that both numbers share as multiples.

Q: What is the relationship between multiples and jumps in the chapter?

In this chapter, "jumps" are the steps or distances an animal or person takes when moving forward, like jumping 3 or 6 steps each time. Multiples are the numbers you get when you multiply a number by 1, 2, 3, and so on. The relationship is that each jump corresponds to a multiple of the number of steps taken. For example, if you jump 3 steps each time, the numbers you land on (3, 6, 9, 12, ...) are multiples of 3. So, jumps help us find multiples on the number line!

Q: Why is 12 a multiple of 1, 2, 3, 4, 6, and 12?

12 is a multiple of 1, 2, 3, 4, 6, and 12 because each of these numbers can divide 12 exactly without leaving any remainder. For example, 12 ÷ 2 = 6, 12 ÷ 3 = 4, and 12 ÷ 4 = 3. When a number can be divided evenly by another number, it means that the second number is a factor of the first, and the first number is a multiple of the second. So, 12 is a multiple of all these numbers because they all divide 12 perfectly.

Q: How can you use multiplication tables to find common multiples?

You can use multiplication tables to find common multiples by looking for numbers that appear in both tables. For example, if you want to find common multiples of 3 and 4, write the tables of 3 (3, 6, 9, 12, 15, ...) and 4 (4, 8, 12, 16, 20, ...). The first number that appears in both tables is 12, so 12 is a common multiple of 3 and 4. Keep looking further in both tables to find more common multiples, like 24, 36, and so on. This way, multiplication tables help you see which numbers are multiples of both numbers.

Q: What misconception do students have about multiples and factors?

Many students think that multiples and factors are the same, but they are different. Factors are numbers that divide another number exactly, like 2 and 3 are factors of 6 because 6 ÷ 2 = 3 and 6 ÷ 3 = 2. Multiples are numbers you get when you multiply a number by 1, 2, 3, and so on, like 3, 6, 9, 12 are multiples of 3. Remember, factors go "inside" a number, while multiples go "beyond" it. Understanding this difference helps in solving problems about numbers better!

Q: ★ 4/5 — Good A: ★ 5/5 — Excellent Overall: ★ 5/5 — Excellent

Q: How do common multiples help in solving jumping or racing problems?

Common multiples are helpful in jumping or racing problems because they show the points where two or more jumpers or racers land together. For example, if one rabbit jumps 4 steps and another jumps 6 steps, their common multiples tell us the steps where both will land at the same time. This helps us know who will reach a certain point first or if they will land together. By finding common multiples, we can solve questions about who reaches a goal first or when they will meet during their jumps.

📖 Chapter 14: Maps and Locations
✅ RAG (8 chunks)LKB: ✅DKB: 25 entries
✅RAG source material found — 8 chunks indexed from textbook PDF.

🎯 What We LearnIntroduction

What You Will Learn

In this lesson, we will learn about directions on a map and how to find our way from one place to another using directions like North, South, East, and West. We will also explore how people and birds find their way during migration.

Simple Explanation

A map shows us where places are located. To find a place on a map, we need to understand directions. The four main directions are North, South, East, and West. When you face the rising Sun in the morning, you are facing East. The opposite direction is West, and the directions left and right are North and South respectively. Knowing directions helps us reach places easily and understand how animals and humans navigate.

Step-by-step Breakdown

  • Cardinal directions: North, South, East, and West are called the four main directions.
  • Facing the Sun: In the morning, the Sun rises in the East. If you face the Sun, you are facing East.
  • Using your hands: If you stand facing East, your left hand points North, and your right hand points South.
  • Finding West: West is directly opposite to East.
  • Using maps: Maps have arrows and symbols to show directions. By reading these, we can find routes from one place to another.
  • Bird migration: Birds find their way during migration by using the Sun, stars, and Earth's magnetic field, which help them determine directions.

Worked Example

Question:

The following data shows the positions of different animals in a zoo map with coordinates:

  • Panda is at (1,1)
  • Tortoise is at (4,1)
  • Deer is at (6,4)
  • Elephant is at (2,6)

Find the route to reach the tortoise from the panda using the grid coordinates, moving only along the rows and columns.

Solution:

  • Step 1: Start at (1,1) where the Panda is located.
  • Step 2: To reach the tortoise at (4,1), move 3 steps south (since row number increases downward).
  • Step 3: The route is: from (1,1) to (4,1). The movement is straight down.

Conceptual interpretation:

The tortoise is directly south of the panda, so the shortest route is moving straight down along the same column.

Common Mistake

A common mistake is trying to move diagonally on the grid. Remember, on a map grid, movement is only along the rows (left/right) or columns (up/down). Diagonal movement is not possible unless specified.

Quick Check Question

If you are standing facing the Sun in the morning, which direction are you facing?

a) North

b) South

c) East

d) West

Summary

  • Directions help us find places on a map.
  • The main directions are North, South, East, and West.
  • Facing the rising Sun means facing East.
  • Birds and animals use the Sun, stars, and Earth's magnetic field to find their way.
  • Use grid coordinates to find shortest routes in maps.
  • Review these key points, then move to the next lesson step when ready.

🧪 Worked ExamplesExamples

What you will learn

In this lesson, we will learn how to find directions on a map and understand how maps help us locate places. We will also see how to read routes and understand the importance of directions like North, South, East, and West.

Simple explanation

A map is a drawing that shows where places are located. To understand a map, we need to know directions. The main directions are North, South, East, and West. Maps often show routes, which are paths from one place to another. By following the directions and routes on a map, we can find the shortest or best way to reach a place.

Step-by-step breakdown

1. Understanding directions:

  • North is the direction where the Sun rises.
  • South is opposite to North.
  • East is to the right when facing the Sun rising.
  • West is to the left.

2. Reading a map:

  • Maps have symbols and keys that tell us what different signs mean.
  • Routes are shown with lines, and their directions help us move from one place to another.

3. Finding routes:

  • To go from one place to another, follow the route on the map, paying attention to the directions.
  • Sometimes, there are multiple routes, and we need to find the shortest or easiest one.

4. Using landmarks and streets:

  • Landmarks like parks, schools, or bus stops help us identify locations.
  • Streets and roads on the map guide us along the route.

Worked example

Question:

The following data shows the route from Ravi’s house to the children’s park on a map. Ravi’s house is at position (3, 2) and the children’s park is at position (7, 5). Draw the route Ravi should take, moving only along the grid lines. Indicate the directions and the number of steps.

Solution:

  • Step 1: Find Ravi’s house at (3, 2).
  • Step 2: Find the children’s park at (7, 5).
  • Step 3: To move from (3, 2) to (7, 2), move horizontally east.
  • Number of steps: 7 - 3 = 4 steps east.
  • Step 4: From (7, 2) to (7, 5), move vertically north.
  • Number of steps: 5 - 2 = 3 steps north.
  • Final route:
  • 4 steps east, then 3 steps north.
  • The route is shortest because it moves directly along the grid lines without extra turns.

This shows how understanding directions and grid positions helps us plan the shortest route.

Common mistake

Many students think they can move diagonally on the map, but maps are based on grid lines. Movement must follow horizontal and vertical lines only, which makes routes longer if not planned properly.

Quick check question

If you start at (2, 3) and want to reach (5, 7), how many steps east and north will you take? Describe the route.

Summary

  • Maps show locations and routes using symbols and directions.
  • The main directions are North, South, East, and West.
  • To find the shortest route, move along grid lines, first horizontally then vertically or vice versa.
  • Understanding directions helps us navigate easily and find places quickly.

Review these key points, then move to the next lesson step when ready.

Q: How can a metric grid be utilized to determine the distance between two points on a map?
  • A metric grid draws evenly spaced grid lines on the map, typically with a unit of 100 m or 1 km.
  • The coordinates of a point are given by its distance from a chosen origin along the two grid axes.
  • The distance between two points is the straight-line length connecting them, which can be measured directly on the map if the map is labelled accurately.
  • By counting the number of grid squares and multiplying by the grid spacing, the map distance is obtained.
  • Convert the map distance to real distance using the map scale or the metric grid spacing given.
  • The grid thus allows quick estimation of distances without drawing a line.

Q: In what way does the legend of a map assist the reader in interpreting map symbols?
  • The legend, or key, explains the symbols used on a map for various features.
  • It lists each symbol and the feature it represents, such as rivers, roads, or schools.
  • The legend helps readers interpret the map correctly by matching symbols to real-world objects.
  • This guidance prevents misidentification of features and ensures accurate navigation.
  • On thematic maps, the legend also shows the colour coding or shading that represents quantitative data.
  • By consulting the legend, the user can decode complex maps and use them effectively.

💡LKB Chip · Worked Examples👁 0 hits
Q: How does the scale of a map help in calculating the actual distance between two places?
  • The scale of a map is the ratio between distances on the map and distances on the ground.
  • For a map with a scale of 1:50,000, one centimetre on the map represents 50,000 centimetres (or 500 metres) on the ground.
  • By multiplying the map distance by the scale factor, the actual distance between two places can be determined.
  • Maps can use a graphic ratio (e.g., 1 cm : 1 km) or a metric scale (e.g., 1 cm = 1 km).
  • The scale allows conversion from map units to real-world units without a ruler if the scale factor is known.
  • When the map distance is measured in centimetres, the ground distance in kilometres is obtained by multiplying with the numeric scale (e.g., 5 cm × 1 km/cm = 5 km).
Q: ★ 4/5 — Good A: ★ 5/5 — Excellent Overall: ★ 5/5 — Excellent
Q: Question does not reference chapter content directly | A: Well-structured, appropriately detailed answer
💡LKB Chip · Worked Examples👁 0 hits
Q: What are the steps involved in using the coordinate method to find the direction from one location to another?
  • The coordinate method assigns each place a pair of numbers (x, y) giving its position on a grid.
  • To find the distance between two places, compute the difference in their x coordinates and their y coordinates.
  • Use the Pythagorean theorem: √[(Δx)² + (Δy)²] gives the map distance between the points.
  • Scale the map distance to ground distance by multiplying by the scale factor.
  • Direction is obtained by calculating the ratio Δy/Δx and converting it to a bearing using trigonometry or the direction table.
  • The resulting direction (e.g., N 10° E) indicates the line of travel from one place to the other.
Q: ★ 4/5 — Good A: ★ 5/5 — Excellent Overall: ★ 5/5 — Excellent
Q: Question does not reference chapter content directly | A: Well-structured, appropriately detailed answer
💡LKB Chip · Worked Examples👁 0 hits
Q: Why is it important to distinguish between cardinal directions and intermediate directions when working with maps?
  • Cardinal directions (N, S, E, W) are the four primary directions that can be used to orient a map.
  • Intermediate directions (NE, SE, NW, SW) fill the gaps between the cardinal points and aid in finer navigation.
  • Using only cardinal directions may lead to ambiguous routes; intermediate directions provide more precise bearings.
  • In map reading, the combination of cardinal and intermediate directions corresponds to angles on a compass.
  • Distinguishing between them ensures consistent interpretation of bearings and distances.
  • Accurate direction knowledge is essential for determining the shortest path between locations.

📌 RecapSummary

Lesson on Recap of Maps and Locations for Grade 5

1. What you will learn

Today, we will review how to read maps, find locations, and understand directions using maps and real-world examples. We will learn how to identify places, follow routes, and find directions like north, south, east, and west. This will help you understand how maps work and how to navigate in your environment.

2. Simple explanation

A map is a drawing that shows the location of places in an area. It uses symbols and a key to help us understand what each symbol means. Directions tell us which way to go—like north, south, east, or west. When we follow routes on a map, we move from one place to another by following the lines and directions. Knowing how to read maps helps us find places, plan routes, and understand our surroundings better.

3. Step-by-step breakdown

  • Understanding maps: Maps are scaled-down pictures of real places. They show roads, parks, buildings, and other important places.
  • Symbols and keys: Maps use symbols like squares, circles, or colors. The key explains what each symbol means.
  • Directions: The four main directions are North, South, East, and West. They help us describe where places are located.
  • Finding routes: To go from one place to another on a map, follow the lines (roads or paths) and use directions to guide you.
  • Using grids: Some maps have a grid with rows and columns. The position of a place is given by a pair of numbers, like (3, 5). The first number is the row, and the second is the column.
  • Real-world application: You can draw your own map of your school or neighborhood, mark hidden objects, and practice finding their locations.

4. Worked example

Let's find the location of the panda on a zoo map with a grid.

  • The panda is at position (1, 1).
  • To reach the panda from the starting point (0, 0), you move:
  • 1 step east (to increase the column number from 0 to 1).
  • 1 step north (to increase the row number from 0 to 1).
  • So, the route is: move 1 step east, then 1 step north.
  • The position (1, 1) is where the panda is located.

This shows how grid coordinates help us locate animals or objects precisely.

5. Common mistake

Many students think that the first number in the grid coordinate is the column instead of the row. Remember:

  • The first number indicates the row (up and down).
  • The second number indicates the column (left and right).

Mixing these up can lead to wrong locations.

6. Quick check question

If the tortoise is at position (4, 1), how many steps do you need to move east to reach it from (1, 1)?

---

7. Summary

Today, we reviewed how to read maps, understand directions, and locate places using grids and symbols. Remember:

  • Maps are scaled drawings of real places.
  • Use the key to understand symbols.
  • Directions like north, south, east, and west help us find our way.
  • Grid coordinates give exact locations.
  • Practice drawing maps and locating objects to improve your map skills.

Review these key points, then move to the next lesson step when ready.

Q: Explain the concept of latitude and longitude and how they help in locating places.
  • Latitude measures north-south distance from the equator.
  • Longitude measures east-west distance from the Greenwich Meridian.
  • Latitude lines are parallel while longitude lines converge at the poles.
  • Coordinates are expressed in degrees, with latitude ranging from 0° to 90°, and longitude from 0° to 180°.
  • A location can be uniquely identified by a combination of latitude and longitude values, e.g., 28° N, 77° E.
  • This grid system helps locate places precisely on the Earth’s surface.

Q: What steps are involved in making a map as discussed in the chapter?
  • Observation of the region is done by field visits or satellite data collection.
  • Data on physical or administrative features are recorded and organized.
  • Preliminary sketches are made on paper or a computer to outline major elements.
  • Symbols, colours, and labels are added following standard conventions.
  • The map is checked for accuracy and consistency with references.
  • The final map is printed or published, with an accompanying legend and scale.

Q: What are the main differences between a topographic map and a political map?
  • A topographic map displays physical features such as mountains, hills, valleys, and bodies of water using contour lines and shading.
  • A topographic map includes contours which indicate elevations and the steepness of the terrain.
  • A political map summarizes how the world country or the country is divided into states, districts, or cities.
  • A political map depicts administrative boundaries and each country or state is differentiated by colours.
  • A political map does not represent terrain or elevation.
  • A topographic map is more detailed and is useful for engineering or natural resource studies.

A map is a picture or drawing that shows the important places and features of a place, like a city, country, or park. It helps us understand where things are and how to find our way from one place to another. Maps use symbols, lines, and colors to show roads, rivers, buildings, and other important places.

Q: How do you find the shortest route on a map?

To find the shortest route on a map, look for the path that has the least distance or the fewest steps to reach your destination. You can do this by counting the number of lines or spaces you need to cross, or by measuring the distance if the map shows scale (like 1 cm = 1 km). Sometimes, there are different routes, so compare them and choose the one with the shortest length or fewest turns. This helps you reach your destination faster and easier!

📖 Chapter 15: Data Through Pictures
✅ RAG (9 chunks)LKB: ✅DKB: 25 entries
✅RAG source material found — 9 chunks indexed from textbook PDF.

🎯 What We LearnIntroduction

Lesson on "What We Learn" in Data Through Pictures

1. What you will learn

In this lesson, we will understand how to interpret data presented through pictures (pictographs). We will learn how pictures can help us quickly understand information, how to read data from pictographs, and how to compare quantities using pictures. We will also explore how to choose appropriate scales for pictographs to represent large data effectively.

---

2. Simple explanation

A pictograph uses pictures or icons to show data instead of numbers. Each picture or icon can represent a certain number of items. This makes it easier to see and compare data at a glance. For example, if one icon shows 5 toys, then 3 icons mean 15 toys. Choosing the right scale (how many items each picture stands for) helps us make accurate and clear pictographs.

---

3. Step-by-step breakdown

Step 1: Understanding the purpose of pictographs

  • Pictographs are used to represent large amounts of data simply.
  • They help us quickly see which item is more or less, or compare quantities.
  • Example: Showing how many toys, board games, or sports items are in a shop.

Step 2: Using icons to represent data

  • Each icon stands for a fixed number of items, called the scale.
  • Example: One icon = 5 toys.
  • To find the total items, count the icons and multiply by the scale.

Step 3: Reading data from pictographs

  • Count the number of icons for each item.
  • Multiply the number of icons by the scale to find the total.
  • Example: If there are 4 icons for toys and each icon = 5 toys, then total toys = 4 × 5 = 20.

Step 4: Choosing scales

  • When data is large, pick a scale that makes the pictograph neat.
  • Example: Instead of 1 icon = 1 item, use 1 icon = 5 items to keep the picture simple.
  • You can choose different scales depending on the data size.

Step 5: Comparing data

  • Use the number of icons to compare quantities.
  • The larger the number of icons, the greater the quantity.

Step 6: Practical activities

  • Making your own pictograph using data from daily life.
  • Choosing suitable scales for different data sets.
  • Interpreting pictographs to answer questions.

---

4. Worked example

Problem:

Joseph Uncle has recorded the number of toys, board games, and sports items in his shop using a pictograph. Each icon represents 5 items.

| Item | Number of icons |

|--------------|----------------|

| Toys | 4 |

| Board games | 3 |

| Sports items | 2 |

Solution:

  • Toys: 4 icons × 5 = 20 toys
  • Board games: 3 icons × 5 = 15 games
  • Sports items: 2 icons × 5 = 10 items

Total play items:

20 + 15 + 10 = 45 items

Explanation:

  • Counted icons for each item.
  • Multiplied by the scale (5) to get actual quantities.
  • Added all to find total.

---

5. Common mistake

Many students forget to multiply the number of icons by the scale. They just count icons as the total, which leads to incorrect answers. Always remember: total items = number of icons × scale.

---

6. Quick check question

Suppose a pictograph shows 6 icons for toys, with each icon representing 4 toys. How many toys are there in total?

---

7. Summary

  • Pictographs use pictures to represent data, making it easier to understand and compare.
  • Each icon stands for a fixed number of items called the scale.
  • To find the total, count icons and multiply by the scale.
  • Choosing the right scale helps in creating clear and accurate pictographs.
  • Practice making and reading pictographs with different data sets to improve your skills.

Review these key points, then move to the next lesson step when ready.

🧪 Worked ExamplesExamples

Lesson on Data Through Pictures: Worked Examples

What you will learn

  • How to interpret pictographs (pictures representing data).
  • How to answer questions based on pictographs.
  • How to use different scales to make pictographs.
  • How to solve real-life data problems with pictures.

Key concepts covered

  • Using icons to represent quantities.
  • Understanding scales (how many items one icon represents).
  • Reading data from pictographs.
  • Comparing quantities using pictographs.
  • Making different pictographs with various scales.

---

Why are pictographs useful?

Pictographs help us understand and compare data easily because pictures are simple and visual. Instead of counting large numbers, we can count icons, which makes data more understandable and quick to interpret.

---

Step-by-step explanation of how to solve pictograph problems

Step 1: Understand the data and the scale

  • Look at the data table (e.g., number of toys, board games, sports items).
  • Check the scale used (e.g., 1 icon = 5 items). This tells us how to convert icons into actual quantities.

Step 2: Read the pictograph

  • Count the number of icons for each item.
  • Multiply the number of icons by the scale to find the total items.

Step 3: Answer questions

  • Use the data from the pictograph and the scale to answer questions about quantities, comparisons, or totals.

Step 4: Make new pictographs (if needed)

  • Choose a scale suitable for your data.
  • Draw icons accordingly to represent the data.

---

Worked Example 1: Joseph Uncle’s Play Items

Given data:

| Item | Number of Items | Scale used: 1 icon = 5 items |

|---------------|------------------|------------------------------|

| Toys | 20 | 4 icons (because 20 ÷ 5 = 4) |

| Board games | 15 | 3 icons (because 15 ÷ 5 = 3) |

| Sports items | 25 | 5 icons (because 25 ÷ 5 = 5) |

Question 1: How many toys does Joseph Uncle have?

Solution:

  • Count the icons for toys: 4 icons.
  • Since 1 icon = 5 toys, total toys = 4 × 5 = 20 toys.

Question 2: How many total play items does Joseph Uncle have?

Solution:

  • Toys: 20
  • Board games: 15
  • Sports items: 25
  • Total = 20 + 15 + 25 = 60 items.

Question 3: Can you make a pictograph with a different scale?

Answer:

  • Yes. For example, if we choose 1 icon = 10 items:
  • Toys: 20 ÷ 10 = 2 icons
  • Board games: 15 ÷ 10 ≈ 1.5 → round to 2 icons or use a different scale.
  • Sports items: 25 ÷ 10 ≈ 2.5 → round to 3 icons.

---

Visualize the pictograph for Joseph Uncle’s data

```visual-json

{"type":"flow","title":"Creating a pictograph with scale 1 icon = 5 items","items":["Toys: 4 icons","Board games: 3 icons","Sports items: 5 icons"],"note":"Each icon represents 5 items"}

```

---

Worked Example 2: Two-Wheelers Passing by Deepti

Data:

  • Monday: 9 icons → 9 × 3 = 27 two-wheelers
  • Wednesday: 6 icons → 6 × 3 = 18 two-wheelers
  • Friday: 10 icons → 10 × 3 = 30 two-wheelers

Questions:

1. Which day had the most two-wheelers?

Answer: Friday (30 two-wheelers).

2. How many two-wheelers in total over three days?

Answer: 27 + 18 + 30 = 75 two-wheelers.

3. How many fewer on Wednesday than Monday?

Answer: 27 - 18 = 9 fewer.

4. How many more on Friday than Wednesday?

Answer: 30 - 18 = 12 more.

---

Practical activity: Making your own pictograph

  • Collect data about your class, like the number of students with different favorite colors.
  • Decide on a scale (e.g., 1 icon = 2 students).
  • Draw the pictograph and answer questions based on it.

---

Common mistakes to avoid

  • Forgetting to multiply icons by the scale to find total quantities.
  • Using inconsistent scales when comparing data.
  • Rounding errors when converting data to icons.
  • Not clearly labeling the scale used.

---

Summary

  • Pictographs use pictures to represent data, making it easier to understand and compare.
  • Always

Q: How can we find the missing population for a category in a pictograph when the total number of icons for that category is known?
  • Multiply the number of icons for the category by the scaling factor to get the population.
  • The scaling factor is already defined in the pictograph description.
  • This calculation converts icon count back to the original data units.
  • The result gives the exact number of people belonging to that category.
  • It confirms that the pictograph represents an accurate frequency distribution.
  • The method is used consistently in worked examples to determine missing data.

Q: What are the steps to convert a bar diagram into a pictograph, as shown in the worked examples?
  • Identify the data values represented by each horizontal bar.
  • Choose a scaling factor that yields a convenient number of icons for every data value.
  • Replace each bar by a corresponding number of icons according to the scaling factor.
  • Label each icon group with its category for clear identification.
  • Verify that the sum of icons matches the total data value.
  • The completed pictograph is a visual frequency distribution of the data.

Q: How do we calculate the percentage of a particular category from a pictograph used in the worked examples?
  • Multiply the number of icons for the category by the scaling factor to get the actual population.
  • Sum the populations of all categories to obtain the total population.
  • Divide the category population by the total population to get the proportion.
  • Multiply the proportion by 100 to convert it to a percentage.
  • Round the percentage to a sensible number of decimal places as required.
  • The percentage provides insight into the relative size of that category.

Q: What method is employed in the worked examples to find the total frequency from a frequency table?
  • List each frequency value corresponding to the categories.
  • Add all the frequency values together.
  • The sum gives the total frequency, which is the overall count of occurrences.
  • This total can be used to compute probabilities or percentages.
  • The addition check validates that no data are omitted.
  • The total frequency is crucial for interpreting the rest of the dataset.

💡LKB Chip · Worked Examples👁 0 hits
Q: How do we determine the scaling factor for a pictograph as demonstrated in the worked examples?
  • The scaling factor represents the number of people contained in one pictograph icon.
  • It is obtained by dividing the largest data value shown by the number of icons used for that value.
  • After calculating, the scaling factor should be a simple integer so that all other categories yield whole icons.
  • This factor is used to interpret the pictograph and to check its accuracy.
  • If an icon count for a category is fractional, the scaling factor is adjusted to eliminate fractions.
  • In the worked example, the scaling factor was determined to be 2 people per icon.

📌 RecapSummary

Lesson on Data Through Pictures (Recap)

---

What you will learn

Today, we will review how to interpret and create pictographs, understand the use of scales, and analyze data presented in pictures. We will also see how pictographs help us compare quantities easily.

---

Simple explanation

A pictograph uses pictures or icons to show data. Each picture can represent a certain number of items. Using pictures makes it easier to understand large amounts of data at a glance. Sometimes, to make a pictograph clear and manageable, we use a scale—meaning each picture stands for a specific number of items, not just one.

---

Step-by-step breakdown

1. Understanding pictographs

  • Pictographs are visual representations of data using pictures.
  • Each picture or icon in a pictograph stands for a certain number of items.
  • Example: One icon = 5 toys.

2. Using scales

  • When data is large, a scale helps simplify the pictograph.
  • Example: Dipesh used one icon for every 5 toys, board games, or sports items.
  • This means if there are 10 toys, the pictograph will show 2 icons (because 10 ÷ 5 = 2).

3. Reading data from pictographs

  • Count the number of icons.
  • Multiply by the scale value to find the total items.
  • Example: 3 icons, each representing 5 toys, means 3 × 5 = 15 toys.

4. Creating your own pictograph

  • Decide on a scale (e.g., 1 icon = 3 items).
  • Draw icons according to the data.
  • Label the icons with the total number of items.

5. Comparing data using pictographs

  • Look at the number of icons for each category.
  • Convert icons into actual numbers using the scale.
  • Compare the totals to analyze which category has more or less.

---

Worked example

Question:

Joseph Uncle has 23 toys, 15 board games, and 18 sports items.

If each icon represents 5 items, how many icons will you draw for each?

Solution:

  • Toys: 23 ÷ 5 = 4.6 → Round to 5 icons (since we can't draw half an icon, we either round or use fractional icons if allowed).
  • Board games: 15 ÷ 5 = 3 icons.
  • Sports items: 18 ÷ 5 = 3.6 → 4 icons.

Final pictograph:

  • Toys: 5 icons
  • Board games: 3 icons
  • Sports items: 4 icons

This helps us visualize the quantities easily.

---

Common mistake

Many students forget to multiply the number of icons by the scale value to find the actual data. Always remember:

Number of icons × scale = total items

---

Quick check question

If a pictograph shows 4 icons, and each icon represents 3 items, how many items are there in total?

---

Summary

  • Pictographs use pictures to represent data.
  • A scale helps simplify large data.
  • Count icons and multiply by the scale to find total items.
  • Comparing pictographs makes data easy to analyze.
  • Practice creating and reading pictographs to improve your understanding.

Review these key points, then move to the next lesson step when ready.

  • A pictograph uses symbols while a bar chart uses bars.\n- In pictographs the number of symbols directly represents quantity; in bar charts the bar length represents quantity.\n- Pictographs are suitable for small data sets with recognizable symbols; bar charts handle larger quantitative values.\n- Bar charts require a clear linear scale, whereas pictographs may use a simple count of symbols.\n- Bar charts can show precise values with labels and axis, whereas pictographs usually convey approximate comparisons.\n- Pictographs often lack axis labels; bar charts always include axes and scales.\n- Bar charts can represent continuous data; pictographs are limited to discrete counts that symbols can represent.

📖 Chapter 2: Fractions

🎯 What We LearnIntroduction

Lesson on "What We Learn" in Fractions (Grade 5)

1. What you will learn

In this lesson, we will understand:

  • How to compare fractions to see which is bigger or smaller.
  • When two fractions are equal (called equivalent fractions).
  • How to find fractions that are greater than or less than a certain value, especially 1/2.
  • The importance of the same whole when comparing fractions.
  • Practical ways to compare fractions using visual aids like number lines and fraction kits.

2. Simple explanation

Fractions tell us parts of a whole. When comparing fractions, we need to see how many parts each fraction represents of the same whole. If the parts are from different wholes, we must make sure the wholes are the same before comparing. Fractions can be equal if they represent the same part of a whole, even if their numbers look different. For example, 1/2 and 2/4 are equal because they both mean half of a whole.

3. Step-by-step breakdown

Comparing Fractions

  • Same Whole: To compare fractions like 1/4 and 3/4, look at the denominator. The larger the denominator, the smaller each part is. Since 4 is the same, compare the numerators: 1 and 3. So, 3/4 is bigger than 1/4.
  • Different Whole: If fractions are from different wholes, we need to make the wholes the same. For example, compare 1/2 and 1/3.
  • Find a common denominator, which is 6.
  • Convert: 1/2 = 3/6, and 1/3 = 2/6.
  • Now compare: 3/6 > 2/6, so 1/2 > 1/3.

Equivalent Fractions

  • What are they? Fractions that look different but mean the same part of a whole.
  • How to find them? Multiply numerator and denominator by the same number.
  • Example: 1/2 = 2/4 (multiply both by 2).
  • Why are they important? They help us compare fractions easily and understand that different fractions can represent the same quantity.

Using Visuals

  • Number line: Place fractions on a number line to see which is farther right (greater) or left (smaller).
  • Fraction kits: Use pieces to physically see how many parts make a whole and compare parts directly.

Practical activity

  • Shade parts of grids or shapes to find fractions like 1/3, 1/4, 2/4, etc., and see which are equal or larger.

4. Worked example

Let's compare 3/4 and 2/3:

  • Find a common denominator: 12 (since 12 is divisible by both 4 and 3).
  • Convert:

3/4 = (3×3)/(4×3) = 9/12

2/3 = (2×4)/(3×4) = 8/12

  • Compare: 9/12 > 8/12, so 3/4 > 2/3.

This shows that 3/4 is bigger than 2/3 because when both are written with the same denominator, the numerator tells us which is larger.

5. Common mistake

Many students think that a bigger numerator always means a bigger fraction. But if the denominators are different, you must compare the size of the parts, not just the numbers. For example, 1/2 is larger than 3/8, even though 3 is bigger than 1, because the parts are different sizes.

6. Quick check question

Compare 4/5 and 3/4. Which one is larger? Use the method of finding a common denominator or visual aids to reason out your answer.

7. Summary

  • To compare fractions, make sure they are from the same whole.
  • Find common denominators or use visual tools like number lines and fraction kits.
  • Fractions are equal if they represent the same part of a whole, even if they look different.
  • Practice comparing fractions regularly to understand their sizes better.
  • Review these key points, then move to the next lesson step when ready.

Q: What are the rules for adding two fractions with the same denominator?
  • The fractions must have a common denominator.
  • Keep the common denominator unchanged.
  • Add the numerators of the two fractions.
  • Write the sum of numerators over the common denominator.
  • Reduce the resulting fraction to its simplest form if possible.
  • This method works only when the denominators are equal.

Q: How can one identify equivalent fractions?
  • Two fractions are equivalent if they represent the same part of a whole.
  • You can obtain equivalent fractions by multiplying or dividing both numerator and denominator by the same non‑zero integer.
  • If the ratio of the numerators is identical to the ratio of the denominators, the fractions are equivalent.
  • Equivalent fractions can be checked by simplifying each fraction to its simplest form.
  • In calculations, using equivalent fractions can make addition or subtraction easier.
  • The concept of equivalent fractions is fundamental for comparing and simplifying fractions.
Q: ★ 5/5 — Excellent A: ★ 5/5 — Excellent Overall: ★ 5/5 — Excellent

Q: What types of fractions are covered in the chapter?
  • Simple fraction is a fraction in which the numerator is less than the denominator.
  • Composite fraction is a fraction in which the numerator is greater than the denominator.
  • Mixed fraction is a combination of a whole number and a simple fraction.
  • Improper fraction is a fraction in which the numerator is equal to or greater than the denominator.
  • One‑third, one‑half, and one‑fourth are common types of fractions found in everyday situations.
  • Equivalent fractions are different fractions that represent the same part of a whole.

💡LKB Chip · What We Learn👁 0 hits
Q: What is a fraction and what are its main parts?
  • A fraction is a number that represents a part of a whole.
  • It is written as two integers one on top of the other separated by a horizontal line.
  • The integer on the top is called the numerator.
  • The integer on the bottom is called the denominator.
  • The denominator shows how many equal parts the whole is divided into.
  • The fraction tells how many of those parts are taken, as indicated by the numerator.
Q: ★ 4/5 — Good A: ★ 5/5 — Excellent Overall: ★ 5/5 — Excellent

💡LKB Chip · What We Learn👁 0 hits
Q: What does the chapter teach about converting fractions to mixed numbers?
  • A mixed number consists of a whole number and a proper fraction.
  • Divide the numerator by the denominator to find the whole number part.
  • The remainder becomes the numerator of the fraction part.
  • The denominator remains unchanged and is less than the numerator in the fraction part.
  • The whole number part is obtained by integer division of numerator by denominator.
  • Mixed numbers are useful for representing parts of a whole that exceed one.
Q: ★ 5/5 — Excellent A: ★ 5/5 — Excellent Overall: ★ 5/5 — Excellent

🧪 Worked ExamplesExamples

Lesson on Comparing Fractions for Grade 5

What you will learn

  • How to compare two fractions to see which one is bigger or smaller.
  • When fractions are equal or not.
  • How to use visual aids like number lines to compare fractions.
  • Understanding equivalent fractions and their importance in comparison.

Simple explanation

Fractions tell us parts of a whole. To compare two fractions, we need to see which one represents a larger part of the whole. If the fractions have the same denominator, we compare the numerators directly. If they have different denominators, we can convert them to equivalent fractions with the same denominator or use a number line for comparison.

Step-by-step breakdown

1. Same Denominator: When fractions have the same denominator, compare the numerators. The larger numerator means the larger fraction.

2. Different Denominators: Convert fractions to equivalent fractions with the same denominator, or compare their size on a number line.

3. Using Number Line: Divide the segment between 0 and 1 into equal parts based on the denominator. Place the fractions on the line to see which is farther from zero.

4. Equivalent Fractions: Fractions like $\frac{1}{2}$ and $\frac{2}{4}$ are equal because they represent the same part of a whole, just written differently.

Worked Example

Question: Compare $\frac{3}{8}$ and $\frac{5}{8}$. Which one is larger?

Step 1: Notice both fractions have the same denominator, 8.

Step 2: Compare the numerators: 3 and 5.

Step 3: Since 5 is greater than 3, $\frac{5}{8}$ is greater than $\frac{3}{8}$.

Answer: $\frac{5}{8} > \frac{3}{8}$.

Conceptual interpretation: When denominators are the same, the fraction with the bigger numerator covers more parts of the whole.

---

Question: Compare $\frac{2}{3}$ and $\frac{3}{4}$. Which is larger?

Step 1: The denominators are different (3 and 4), so convert to equivalent fractions with the same denominator.

Step 2: Find the least common denominator (LCD) of 3 and 4, which is 12.

Step 3: Convert $\frac{2}{3}$ to twelfths: $\frac{2}{3} = \frac{8}{12}$.

Step 4: Convert $\frac{3}{4}$ to twelfths: $\frac{3}{4} = \frac{9}{12}$.

Step 5: Compare the numerators: 8 and 9.

Step 6: Since 9 is greater than 8, $\frac{3}{4}$ is larger.

Answer: $\frac{3}{4} > \frac{2}{3}$.

---

Common mistake

Many students compare fractions by only looking at the numerators or denominators without converting or visualizing. Remember, fractions with different denominators need to be converted to a common denominator or visualized on a number line for accurate comparison.

Quick check question

Compare $\frac{4}{9}$ and $\frac{5}{12}$. Which one is larger? Explain your reasoning.

Summary

  • To compare fractions, check if they have the same denominator.
  • If yes, compare numerators directly.
  • If no, find equivalent fractions with a common denominator or use a number line.
  • Remember, fractions are equal if they are equivalent, even if written differently.
  • Use visual tools like number lines for better understanding.

Review these key points, then move to the next lesson step when ready.

  • Keep the fraction balanced by performing the same multiplication for numerator and denominator.
  • The new fraction is equivalent to the original because the multiplied factors cancel out.

📌 RecapSummary

Lesson on Comparing Fractions

What you will learn

Today, we will learn how to compare two fractions to see which one is bigger or smaller. We will understand when one fraction is greater than, less than, or equal to another. We will also learn how to compare fractions with the same numerator or the same denominator.

Simple explanation

Fractions tell us parts of a whole. To compare two fractions, we need to see how much of the whole each fraction represents. If the parts are from the same whole, we compare the size of the parts or the number of parts. If the wholes are different, we need to make the parts equal or compare them on a number line.

Step-by-step breakdown

1. Same Whole, Different Parts:

  • When fractions have the same denominator (bottom number), compare the numerators (top number).
  • The larger numerator means the larger fraction.
  • Example: $\frac{3}{7}$ and $\frac{5}{7}$, since 5 > 3, $\frac{5}{7}$ is greater.

2. Different Whole, Same Parts:

  • When fractions have the same numerator but different denominators, compare the size of the parts.
  • The larger the denominator, the smaller each part.
  • Example: $\frac{1}{4}$ and $\frac{1}{2}$, since 1/2 is bigger.

3. Different Whole and Parts:

  • Convert fractions to have the same denominator or compare on a number line.
  • Use cross-multiplication: compare $a \times d$ and $b \times c$ for fractions $\frac{a}{b}$ and $\frac{c}{d}$.
  • If $a \times d > b \times c$, then $\frac{a}{b} > \frac{c}{d}$.

4. Using Number Line:

  • Divide the segment from 0 to 1 into equal parts based on the denominator.
  • Mark the fractions and see which is farther to the right (greater).

Worked example

Question:

Compare $\frac{3}{8}$ and $\frac{5}{8}$ using the steps above.

Step 1:

Both fractions have the same denominator, 8.

Step 2:

Compare the numerators: 3 and 5.

Step 3:

Since 5 > 3, $\frac{5}{8}$ is greater than $\frac{3}{8}$.

Answer:

$\frac{3}{8} < \frac{5}{8}$

This shows that when the denominators are the same, the fraction with the larger numerator is bigger.

Common mistake

Many students think that a bigger numerator always means a bigger fraction, but this is only true when the denominators are the same. When denominators are different, you must compare the size of the parts or convert to a common denominator.

Quick check question

Compare $\frac{2}{5}$ and $\frac{3}{7}$. Which one is larger? Explain your reasoning.

Summary

  • To compare fractions, first check if they have the same denominator.
  • If yes, compare the numerators.
  • If no, compare the size of the parts or use cross-multiplication.
  • Use a number line for visual comparison.
  • Remember, the key is understanding what each fraction represents in parts of a whole.

Review these key points, then move to the next lesson step when ready.

  • Subtraction of fractions with different denominators also requires a common denominator.
  • Find the least common denominator (LCD) for the given fractions.
  • Adjust each fraction to have the LCD by multiplying numerator and denominator appropriately.
  • Subtract the new numerator of the second fraction from that of the first.
  • Write the result over the common denominator.
  • If the result is negative, express it as a negative fraction or a mixed number with a negative whole part.
  • Use examples in the chapter to follow the step‑by‑step procedure.

Q: Describe the method for adding fractions with different denominators as taught in the chapter.
  • To add fractions, first find a common denominator for the fractions.
  • The least common denominator (LCD) is the smallest number that is divisible by each denominator.
  • Once the LCD is found, adjust each fraction by multiplying the numerator and denominator by a factor that gives the LCD.
  • After adjustment, the fractions have the same denominator and can be added by adding their numerators.
  • The resulting numerator is divided by the common denominator to give the sum.
  • If the numerator is larger than the denominator, the sum can be expressed as a mixed number.
  • This procedure is illustrated by examples in the chapter to aid understanding.

Q: How does one make two fractions equivalent and what is the significance of equivalent fractions?
  • Two fractions are equivalent if they represent the same part of a whole.
  • Equivalent fractions can be obtained by multiplying or dividing both numerator and denominator by the same non‑zero integer.
  • Multiplication of numerator and denominator by the same factor enlarges each part equally.
  • Division of numerator and denominator by the same factor simplifies the fraction.
  • Equivalent fractions have the same value although they look different.
  • In the book, the ratio of the parts of the numerator to denominator remains unchanged.
  • Multiplying each part by a common integer preserves the relative size of the fractional part.

Q: What are the key components of a fraction and how are they identified?
  • A number written with a horizontal line dividing two integers is a fraction.
  • The integer above the line is called the numerator.
  • The integer below the line is called the denominator.
  • The denominator must be a positive integer not equal to zero.
  • The numerator can be any integer, positive, negative, or zero.
  • Fractions represent parts of a whole or parts of a set of equal elements.
  • Proper fractions have numerators less than denominators; improper fractions have numerators greater than or equal to denominators.
  • Mixed numbers combine a whole number with a proper fraction.

Q: What is a fraction?

A fraction is a way to show parts of a whole. It has two parts: the numerator (top number) which tells how many parts you have, and the denominator (bottom number) which tells how many parts the whole is divided into. For example, in 1/2, the 1 is the numerator and shows one part, and the 2 is the denominator, showing the whole divided into 2 parts. Fractions help us understand and compare parts of things, like slices of pizza or pieces of chocolate.

Q: What is the difference between proper and improper fractions?

A proper fraction is a fraction where the numerator (top number) is smaller than the denominator (bottom number), like 3/4 or 2/5. An improper fraction is when the numerator is equal to or larger than the denominator, like 7/4 or 5/5. Proper fractions always represent less than 1, while improper fractions can be equal to or more than 1. For example, 3/4 is proper, and 7/4 is improper.

Q: How do you find a common denominator for fractions?

To find a common denominator for fractions, you need to find a number that both denominators can divide into evenly. This number is called the least common multiple (LCM) of the denominators. Once you find the LCM, you change each fraction so that they both have this number as their denominator. For example, to compare 1/3 and 1/4, the LCM of 3 and 4 is 12. So, you convert 1/3 to 4/12 and 1/4 to 3/12. Now, both fractions have the same denominator, and you can easily compare or add them!

Q: Can two different fractions be equal? How?

Yes, two different fractions can be equal. For example, 1/2 and 2/4 are equal because they both represent the same part of a whole. You can see this by dividing a cake into 2 parts and taking 1 part, or dividing it into 4 parts and taking 2 parts; both ways, you get the same amount. So, even if the numbers look different, the fractions can be equal if they show the same part of a whole.

For example, 3/2 means 3 parts out of 2, which is more than 1 whole. So, fractions can be less than, equal to, or greater than 1 depending on the numbers.

Q: How do you determine which fraction is larger: 3/4 or 2/3?

To compare 3/4 and 2/3, you can find a common denominator or convert them to decimals. Using the common denominator method, the least common denominator of 4 and 3 is 12.

  • Convert 3/4 to twelfths: multiply numerator and denominator by 3 → 3/4 = 9/12
  • Convert 2/3 to twelfths: multiply numerator and denominator by 4 → 2/3 = 8/12

Since 9/12 is bigger than 8/12, 3/4 is larger than 2/3.

Alternatively, converting to decimals:

  • 3/4 = 0.75
  • 2/3 ≈ 0.67

Since 0.75 > 0.67, 3/4 is larger.

Q: Why does 2/4 equal 1/2?

Q: How many 1/8 pieces make 1/2?

To find out how many 1/8 pieces make 1/2, we need to see how many times 1/8 fits into 1/2.

Since 1/2 is the same as 4/8 (because 1/2 = 4/8), we divide 4/8 by 1/8.

Dividing fractions is like multiplying by the reciprocal, so:

(4/8) ÷ (1/8) = 4/8 × 8/1 = 4 × 1 = 4.

Therefore, 4 pieces of 1/8 make 1/2.

Q: When can we say 1/2 of something is bigger than 1/3?

We can say that 1/2 of something is bigger than 1/3 of it when we are talking about the same whole or object. Since dividing into fewer parts (2 parts) makes each part bigger than dividing into more parts (3 parts), 1/2 of the same thing is always bigger than 1/3 of it. For example, if you have a chocolate bar, half of it is bigger than a third of it.

Q: How do you make a whole using different fraction pieces?

📖 Chapter 3: Angles as Turns
✅ RAG (9 chunks)LKB: ✅DKB: 0 entries
✅RAG source material found — 9 chunks indexed from textbook PDF.

🎯 What We LearnIntroduction

What We Will Learn

Today, we will understand what turns are in angles, how to measure them, and how they relate to real-world objects like clocks and fans. We will learn how to divide a circle into parts to measure turns, and how these turns help us understand angles.

---

Simple Explanation

Imagine turning your body or a wheel. The amount you turn is called an "angle." When you turn a little, it's an "acute" angle; when you turn a lot, it's an "obtuse" angle; and when you turn exactly right, it's a "right" angle. Turns are measured in parts of a circle, called "fractions of turns," like 1/4 turn, 1/2 turn, or 1/8 turn. These fractions help us understand how much of a full circle you have turned.

---

Step-by-step Breakdown

1. Understanding Turns as Fractions of a Circle

  • A full circle is one complete turn, called 1 turn.
  • Half a turn (1/2) means turning halfway around.
  • A quarter turn (1/4) is turning a quarter of the circle, like the corner of a square.
  • Smaller parts like 1/8 or 1/12 are even smaller turns.

2. Making a Circle into Parts

  • Fold a circle into equal parts (like cutting a pizza).
  • For example, fold it into 8 parts to find 1/8 of a turn.
  • Attach a straw at the center to show the direction of turn.

3. Measuring Angles Using Parts of a Circle

  • Mark the starting point.
  • Turn the straw to show 1/8, 1/4, 1/2 turns, etc.
  • Use these marks to measure angles in shapes or objects.

4. Using the Angle Measuring Tools

  • Cut out parts of the circle for different fractions.
  • Paste them on paper to create a tool to measure angles in real objects.

5. Relating Turns to Real-World Objects

  • The minute hand of a clock makes turns of 1/4, 1/2, or 1/12 of a circle as time passes.
  • Moving a fan or opening a tap involves turns, which can be measured as fractions of a circle.

---

Worked Example

Suppose you want to measure how much a clock's minute hand moves when it shows 15 minutes past the hour.

  • The full circle of the clock is 60 minutes.
  • Moving from 12 to 3 o'clock is 15 minutes.
  • 15 minutes is a quarter of 60 minutes, so the minute hand makes a 1/4 turn.
  • In terms of turns, this is a 1/4 turn of the circle.
  • If you made a circle into 8 parts (each 1/8 turn), 1/4 turn is equal to 2/8 parts.

This shows that the minute hand moves a quarter of a full turn in 15 minutes.

---

Common Mistake

Many students think that angles are only about sharp corners. Remember, angles also describe how much something has turned around a point, like the hands of a clock or a spinning wheel. Always think in terms of turns or fractions of a circle.

---

Quick Check Question

If the minute hand of a clock moves from 12 to 6, what fraction of a full turn has it made?

Hint: Think about how many minutes have passed and relate it to the full 60 minutes.

---

Summary

Today, we learned that turns are parts of a circle, measured as fractions like 1/4, 1/2, or 1/8. These fractions help us measure angles in shapes, clocks, and everyday objects. Making a circle into parts helps us understand how much something has turned. Practice measuring angles in objects around you using these fractions to become more confident. Review these key points, then move to the next lesson step when ready.

🧪 Worked ExamplesExamples

Lesson on Worked Examples: Angles as Turns

1. What you will learn

In this lesson, we will learn how to measure angles in terms of turns, understand how different fractions of turns relate to angles, and practice solving problems involving these concepts. We will also learn how to use our homemade angle measuring tools to verify our guesses.

2. Simple explanation

Angles can be thought of as turns around a point. When you turn a certain fraction of a full circle, that is called an angle. For example, a quarter turn (1/4) of a circle is a right angle, and a half turn (1/2) is a straight angle. We measure angles in terms of these turns, and sometimes combine fractions to find the total angle.

3. Step-by-step breakdown

  • Understanding turns:

A full turn is 360°, which means turning all the way around once.

Half a turn (1/2) is 180°, a straight line.

A quarter turn (1/4) is 90°, a right angle.

Smaller turns like 1/8 or 1/12 are fractions of these.

  • Using the angle measuring tool:

We make a circle from tracing paper, fold it into equal parts (like 8), and mark the starting point.

By turning the straw or pencil by fractions like 1/8, 1/4, or 1/12 of the circle, we can measure angles.

  • Guess and verify:

Before measuring, guess the size of an angle or turn in terms of fractions.

Use your tool to check if your guess is correct.

  • Adding turns:

When multiple turns are made, add their fractions to find the total angle.

For example, two 1/8 turns make a 1/4 turn because $ \frac{1}{8} + \frac{1}{8} = \frac{2}{8} = \frac{1}{4} $.

  • Relating turns to real objects:

The minute hand of a clock makes a 1/12 turn in 5 minutes, which is 30°.

Moving the hand by different fractions of a turn corresponds to different minutes.

4. Worked example

Problem:

A clock's minute hand moves from 12 o'clock to 3 o'clock.

Question: How many turns has the minute hand made?

Step 1:

  • From 12 to 3, the minute hand has moved 3 hours.
  • Each hour is 5 minutes, so 3 hours = 15 minutes.
  • In terms of turns, the minute hand moves 1/4 of a full turn because:

$ \frac{15}{60} = \frac{1}{4} $ of the clock's full circle.

Step 2:

  • So, the minute hand makes 1/4 turn when moving from 12 to 3.
  • This is a quarter turn, which equals 90°.

Answer:

The minute hand makes a 1/4 turn or quarter turn.

Conceptual reasoning:

  • Since a full turn is 60 minutes, moving 15 minutes is a quarter of the full turn.
  • Fractions of turns help us understand how much the hand has moved in degrees.

5. Common mistake

Many students think that a full turn is always 360°, but they forget that fractions like 1/8 or 1/12 are parts of this full turn. Remember, fractions of a turn directly relate to degrees:

  • 1/4 turn = 90°
  • 1/8 turn = 45°
  • 1/12 turn = 30°

6. Quick check question

If the minute hand of a clock moves by 10 minutes, what fraction of a full turn has it made?

(Answer: $ \frac{10}{60} = \frac{1}{6} $ of a full turn)

7. Summary

  • Angles can be measured as fractions of a full turn, with 1/4, 1/2, and 1/8 being common fractions.
  • Use your homemade tools to verify guesses about angles.
  • Adding fractions of turns helps find the total angle.
  • Relate fractions of turns to real-world objects like clock hands to understand their movement better.
  • Review these key points, then move to practice problems when ready.

  • The definition serves as the foundation for solving all worked examples in the chapter.

Q: How can one find a missing turn when two turns in a sequence are known?

  • By subtracting the sum of the known turns from 360°, the missing turn is obtained.
  • This principle is applied in examples where a final turn is unknown.
  • It relies on the fact that completing a full rotation returns to the starting position.
  • The calculation uses basic arithmetic addition and subtraction of directed angles.
  • The missing turn is always expressed in the same units as the given angles.

  • This property is used to find opposite turns in the worked examples.
  • The sign change is essential for computing the resulting turn when a direction change is involved.

  • It ensures that the net effect of a rotation is consistent regardless of the number of full revolutions.

📌 RecapSummary

Lesson on Angles as Turns – Recap

1. What you will learn

Today, we will review the concept of angles as turns, focusing on understanding how turns are measured in parts of a circle, called "fractions of a turn." We will learn how to make and measure these turns using simple tools and relate them to real-world objects and movements.

2. Simple explanation

Imagine turning your body or a straw. The amount you turn is called an angle. When you turn a little, it’s called an acute angle; when you turn exactly right, it’s a right angle; and when you turn more than right but less than a full circle, it’s an obtuse angle. We measure these turns in parts of a circle called "fractions of a turn," like 1/4, 1/2, or 1/8 of a full turn.

3. Step-by-step breakdown

  • Full circle = 1 turn

This is a complete rotation, like turning around once completely.

  • Half turn = 1/2 turn

Turning halfway around, like when you turn to face the opposite direction.

  • Quarter turn = 1/4 turn

Turning a quarter of the circle, like turning the corner of a square.

  • Other fractions:
  • 1/8 turn = small turn, like a tiny step.
  • 3/8 turn = more than a quarter but less than a half.
  • Making angle measuring tools:
  • Cut a circle from tracing paper.
  • Fold it into equal parts (like 4, 8, or 12).
  • Attach a straw at the center.
  • Mark starting point and turns (like 1/4, 1/8).
  • Cut out parts to measure angles.
  • Measuring turns:
  • Use the tools to see how much you turn objects or yourself.
  • Recognize that turns like 1/4 or 1/8 correspond to specific angles.
  • Relating turns to angles:
  • 1/4 turn = 90° (right angle)
  • 1/2 turn = 180° (straight line)
  • 1/8 turn = 45° (acute angle)

4. Worked example

Suppose you turn a straw by 1/4 of a circle.

  • Since a full circle is 360°,
  • 1/4 of a turn = $ \frac{1}{4} \times 360^\circ = 90^\circ $
  • This is a right angle, which is exactly the angle in a corner of a square or rectangle.

Now, if you turn by 1/8 of a circle:

  • $ \frac{1}{8} \times 360^\circ = 45^\circ $
  • This is an acute angle, smaller than a right angle.

5. Common mistake

Many students think that a 1/2 turn is only 180°, but they forget that it is exactly half of 360°, which makes it 180°. Also, some students confuse the fractions of turns with the actual degree measures, so it’s important to remember the relationship:

  • 1/4 turn = 90°
  • 1/2 turn = 180°
  • 1/8 turn = 45°

6. Quick check question

If you make a turn of 3/8 of a full circle, what is the measure of this angle in degrees?

a) 135°

b) 90°

c) 180°

d) 45°

(Think about how many degrees are in a full circle and multiply by 3/8.)

7. Summary

Today, we reviewed how turns are measured in parts of a circle called fractions of a turn, such as 1/4, 1/8, and 1/2. We learned how to create simple tools to measure these turns and relate them to angles like right, straight, and acute angles. Remember, a full turn is 360°, and fractions of a turn help us understand how objects and ourselves rotate in everyday life.

Next, practice making your own angle measuring tools and try measuring different turns around you!

  • Subtracting one full turn (360°) leaves a remainder of 180°.
  • 180° is exactly one half of a full turn.
  • Thus the remainder angle is 180° = 1/2 turn.
  • After completing 1.5 turns, the object faces opposite to its starting direction.
  • A rotation of 1.5 turns passes through a quarter turn, a half turn and another quarter turn.

🎯 What We LearnIntroduction

Lesson on "What We Learn" from Chapter 4: We the Travellers — II

1. What you will learn

In this lesson, we will understand how to make the sums of two groups of numbers equal by interchanging pairs of numbers. We will also learn how to subtract large numbers, estimate sums and differences, and recognize patterns in numbers. These skills help us solve real-world problems like calculating distances, costs, and comparing quantities.

---

2. Simple explanation

Imagine you have two groups of numbers. When you add all the numbers in each group, sometimes the sums are not equal. Your task is to swap some numbers between the groups so that both groups have the same total sum. This helps us understand how numbers work together and how changing one part affects the whole.

---

3. Step-by-step breakdown

Making Sums Equal by Interchanging Numbers

  • Look at the two groups of numbers.
  • Find the total sum of each group.
  • Think about swapping pairs of numbers from one group to the other.
  • Each swap changes the sums; the goal is to find the minimum swaps needed to make the sums equal.
  • To check if a swap will work, compare the difference in sums and see if swapping certain numbers balances them out.

Example:

Suppose group A has 2, 5, 7 and group B has 3, 6, 8.

  • Sum of A = 2 + 5 + 7 = 14
  • Sum of B = 3 + 6 + 8 = 17
  • Difference = 17 - 14 = 3
  • To balance, swap a number from A with one from B that makes the sums equal.

Subtracting Large Numbers

  • Write the numbers in columns, aligning digits by place value.
  • Subtract digit by digit, borrowing when needed.
  • Check your answer by adding the difference back to the smaller number.

Estimating Sums and Differences

  • Round numbers to nearest hundreds or thousands to estimate.
  • Compare the estimated sum or difference with the actual calculation for quick checks.

Recognizing Patterns in Numbers

  • Consecutive numbers are numbers that follow one after another, like 1, 2, 3.
  • The sum of consecutive numbers follows specific patterns, e.g., 1 + 2 + 3 = 6.
  • Patterns help us find sums quickly without adding all numbers.

---

4. Worked example

Making Sums Equal:

Group A: 2, 5, 7

Group B: 3, 6, 8

  • Sum of A = 14
  • Sum of B = 17
  • Difference = 3

Find a swap:

  • Swap 2 from A with 3 from B:
  • New sum of A = 14 - 2 + 3 = 15
  • New sum of B = 17 - 3 + 2 = 16
  • Not equal yet, try swapping 5 and 6:
  • New sum of A = 14 - 5 + 6 = 15
  • New sum of B = 17 - 6 + 5 = 16
  • Swap 7 and 8:
  • New sum of A = 14 - 7 + 8 = 15
  • New sum of B = 17 - 8 + 7 = 16
  • No simple swap makes them equal, so try swapping 5 and 8:
  • Sum A = 14 - 5 + 8 = 17
  • Sum B = 17 - 8 + 5 = 14
  • Now, swap 2 and 3:
  • Sum A = 17 - 2 + 3 = 18
  • Sum B = 14 - 3 + 2 = 13
  • No luck here; so, the key is to find pairs where the difference in their values equals half the total difference.

---

5. Common mistake

A common mistake is to swap the largest or smallest numbers without considering how the swap affects the total sums. Remember, the goal is to balance the sums with the least swaps, and not just swap randomly.

---

6. Quick check question

Suppose group A has 4 and 6, and group B has 3 and 7. If you swap 4 and 3:

  • What will be the new sums of each group?
  • Will the sums be equal? Why or why not?

---

7. Summary

  • To make sums of two groups equal, consider swapping pairs of numbers that balance the total.
  • Subtract large numbers carefully by aligning digits and borrowing.
  • Estimate sums and differences to check your work quickly.
  • Recognize patterns in numbers, especially with consecutive numbers, to find sums faster.
  • Practice these skills with real-world problems like distances, costs, and quantities.

Review these key points, then move to the next lesson step when ready.

Q: How does one calculate the perimeter of a rectangle using grid paper?
  • Measure the length of one side by counting the grid squares along that side.
  • Measure the adjacent side in the same way.
  • Add the lengths of both adjacent sides and multiply by 2.
  • The sum gives the perimeter in grid units.
  • Each side length measured in squares corresponds to 1 unit.
  • The obtained perimeter is expressed in unit length.

Q: How do you graph a point whose coordinates are (3, 4) on a standard grid?
  • Identify the origin O at (0,0).
  • Move three units to the right along the horizontal axis to the x‑value 3.
  • From that point, move four units upward along the vertical axis to the y‑value 4.
  • Place a dot where the horizontal and vertical lines intersect.
  • Label the dot with its coordinate (3, 4).
  • This dot represents the point in the coordinate plane.

Q: How can you find the distance between two points that have integer coordinates on a grid paper?
  • If the two points share the same x‑coordinate or y‑coordinate, the distance equals the difference along the other axis.
  • For points (x₁,y₁) and (x₂,y₂), compute the horizontal difference |x₂−x₁|.
  • Compute the vertical difference |y₂−y₁|.
  • Use the Pythagoras theorem: distance = √(horizontal² + vertical²).
  • When the distance turns out to be an integer, take the square root to get the exact value.
  • If horizontal and vertical differences are zero, the points coincide and the distance is zero.

Q: What is the role of the origin in a standard Cartesian coordinate plane?
  • The origin is marked as O(0,0) where the horizontal and vertical axes intersect.
  • It serves as the reference point for all coordinates.
  • Every point on the plane can be expressed relative to the origin.
  • The origin lies on both the horizontal and vertical axes.
  • It is the point where the x‑coordinate equals 0 and the y‑coordinate equals 0.
  • All other points are located by counting units along the axes from the origin.

Q: How can you determine the area of a rectangle drawn on a grid paper?
  • Draw the rectangle with edges parallel to the axes.
  • Count the number of grid squares inside the rectangle.
  • Each grid square represents 1 unit².
  • The total count gives the area in unit².
  • The side lengths correspond to the number of squares along each side.
  • Multiplying the side lengths yields the area of the rectangle.

🧪 Worked ExamplesExamples

Chapter 4: We the Travellers — II: Worked Examples

What you will learn

In this lesson, we will learn how to solve problems involving addition and subtraction of large numbers, focusing on methods that make calculations easier and quicker. We will explore how to find missing numbers in equations, compare sums, and understand patterns in sums of consecutive numbers.

Simple explanation

When working with large numbers, it’s important to align digits properly and understand place value. We can use strategies like estimating, breaking numbers into smaller parts, and recognizing patterns to solve problems efficiently. These methods help us avoid mistakes and save time, especially in exams.

Step-by-step breakdown

1. Align numbers correctly: Write numbers so that ones, tens, hundreds, etc., are in the correct columns.

2. Use regrouping (carrying/borrowing): When the sum of digits exceeds 9 or the subtraction digit is larger, regroup by carrying over or borrowing.

3. Estimate before calculating: Make an educated guess to check if your answer makes sense.

4. Identify patterns: Recognize recurring patterns in sums and differences to simplify calculations.

5. Find missing numbers: Use inverse operations (addition/subtraction) to find unknowns in equations.

6. Compare sums: Observe how sums of consecutive numbers relate to each other, noticing differences and patterns.

Worked example

Question:

The sum of two large numbers is 1301. One number is 839. Find the other number.

Step 1:

Write the problem as an equation:

\[ \text{Other number} + 839 = 1301 \]

Step 2:

To find the other number, subtract 839 from 1301:

\[ \text{Other number} = 1301 - 839 \]

Step 3:

Align the digits properly and subtract:

1301

  • 839

______

Step 4:

Subtract units: 1 - 9 cannot be done, so borrow 1 ten from the hundreds place (which is 0, so borrow from thousands):

  • Borrow 1 thousand (which is 1000) and convert it into 10 hundreds, then borrow 1 hundred (which is 100) for the hundreds place.
  • Now, the hundreds digit becomes 10, and the thousands digit reduces by 1.

Step 5:

Perform subtraction step-by-step:

  • Units: 11 - 9 = 2
  • Tens: 0 (after borrowing) - 3 cannot do, borrow 1 hundred (which is 100) from the thousands place:

The hundreds digit becomes 10, and the thousands digit reduces by 1.

  • Now, tens: 10 - 3 = 7
  • Hundreds: 10 - 8 = 2
  • Thousands: 0 (after borrowing) - 0 = 0

Step 6:

Final answer: 462

Conclusion:

The other number is 462.

This method of subtraction ensures accuracy with large numbers by carefully aligning digits and using regrouping.

Common mistake

A common mistake is to forget to borrow when subtracting larger digits from smaller ones, leading to incorrect answers. Always check if the top digit is smaller than the bottom digit in each column and borrow if needed.

Quick check question

If the sum of two numbers is 1500 and one number is 987, what is the other number? Try to find the answer by subtracting.

Summary

  • Properly align large numbers before addition or subtraction.
  • Use regrouping (borrowing and carrying) to handle larger digits.
  • Recognize patterns in sums of consecutive numbers to simplify calculations.
  • Practice finding missing numbers by reversing operations.
  • Always verify your answers by estimating or checking calculations.

Review these key points, then move to the next lesson step when ready.

📌 RecapSummary

Lesson on Making Sums Equal by Interchanging Numbers

What you will learn

Today, we will learn how to make the sums of two groups of numbers equal by swapping pairs of numbers between them, using the least number of moves. We will understand the reasoning behind this process and practice with examples.

Simple explanation

Imagine you have two groups of numbers, and you want their total sums to be the same. You can do this by exchanging some numbers from one group with numbers from the other group. Our goal is to find the best pairs to swap so that the sums become equal, and we do this with the fewest swaps possible.

Step-by-step breakdown

1. Calculate the sums of both groups.

2. Identify pairs of numbers—one from each group—that, when swapped, will bring the sums closer or make them equal.

3. Swap the pairs and recalculate the sums.

4. Repeat the process until the sums are equal or as close as possible with minimal swaps.

5. Choose the best pair to swap by checking how much the sums change with each potential swap.

Worked example

Question:

Group A: 2, 11, 3, 13, 9

Group B: 5, 77, 2, 15, 4

Find the pair of numbers to swap so that the sums of the groups become equal with the least moves.

Step 1:

Calculate the sum of Group A:

2 + 11 + 3 + 13 + 9 = 38

Calculate the sum of Group B:

5 + 77 + 2 + 15 + 4 = 103

Step 2:

Find the difference:

103 - 38 = 65

Step 3:

To make the sums equal, swapping a pair of numbers should reduce this difference.

When we swap a number \(a\) from Group A with a number \(b\) from Group B, the new sums will be:

New sum of Group A: \(38 - a + b\)

New sum of Group B: \(103 - b + a\)

The goal is:

\(|(38 - a + b) - (103 - b + a)| = 0\) or as close as possible.

Simplify the difference:

\[(38 - a + b) - (103 - b + a) = 38 - a + b - 103 + b - a = (38 - 103) + 2b - 2a = -65 + 2(b - a)\]

Set this close to zero:

\(-65 + 2(b - a) \approx 0\)

=> \(2(b - a) \approx 65\)

=> \(b - a \approx 32.5\)

Step 4:

Find pairs where \(b - a \approx 33\).

Possible pairs:

  • Swap 2 (from Group A) with 77 (from Group B):

\(b - a = 77 - 2 = 75\) (too high)

  • Swap 3 with 77:

\(77 - 3 = 74\)

  • Swap 9 with 77:

\(77 - 9 = 68\)

  • Swap 11 with 77:

\(77 - 11 = 66\) (close to 33, but still high)

  • Swap 13 with 77:

\(77 - 13 = 64\)

Check the effect of swapping 11 and 77:

New sums:

A: 38 - 11 + 77 = 104

B: 103 - 77 + 11 = 37

Difference: 104 - 37 = 67 (not equal but closer)

Alternatively, swap 9 and 77:

A: 38 - 9 + 77 = 106

B: 103 - 77 + 9 = 35

Difference: 71 (worse)

Step 5:

Swapping 11 and 77 gets us closer.

Answer:

Swap 11 from Group A with 77 from Group B.

Now, the sums are 104 and 37, which are not equal but the closest possible with one swap.

Summary:

Swapping 11 and 77 reduces the difference significantly. For perfect equality, more swaps or different pairs might be needed, but this was the minimal move to get closer.

Common mistake

A common mistake is to swap the largest number in one group with the smallest in the other without checking how it affects the sums. Always calculate the new sums after swapping to see if it helps.

Quick check question

Suppose you have two groups: Group A: 4, 6, 8 and Group B: 10, 12, 14. What pair of numbers would you swap to make their sums equal? Think about how the sums change with each swap.

Summary

  • To make sums equal by swapping, first find the total sums.
  • Identify pairs where swapping reduces the difference.
  • Calculate the new sums after each potential swap.
  • Choose the pair that minimizes the difference.
  • Practice with different examples to master this technique.

Review these key points, then move to the next lesson step when ready.
